- **nh-principle-attack-the-premise**: Apply when two or more fixes that share one premise have failed the same gate. - **nh-principle-build-the-lever**: Apply to any non-trivial work, not just bulk work: edits, migrations, analyses, checks. ([neohaskell/skills](https://github.com/neohaskell/skills/tree/HEAD/skills/nh-principle-build-the-lever))
- **nh-principle-design-for-throughput**: Apply when designing, implementing or reviewing NeoHaskell framework data structures, service flows, concurrency, storage, codecs or generated APIs. ([neohaskell/skills](https://github.com/neohaskell/skills/tree/HEAD/skills/nh-principle-design-for-throughput))
- **nh-principle-encode-lessons-in-structure**: Apply when you catch yourself writing the same instruction a second time, or notice a recurring correction. ([neohaskell/skills](https://github.com/neohaskell/skills/tree/HEAD/skills/nh-principle-encode-lessons-in-structure))
- **nh-principle-exhaust-the-design-space**: Apply when facing a novel UI interaction or architectural decision with no precedent in the codebase. - **nh-principle-experience-first**: Apply when product, UX, or feature-scope tradeoffs come up. - **nh-principle-fix-root-causes**: Apply when debugging. Trace each symptom back to the first place the system went wrong and fix it there. - **nh-principle-foundational-thinking**: Apply before writing logic: choosing core types and data structures, sequencing scaffold-vs-feature work, asking what concurrent actors share. - **nh-principle-guard-the-context-window**: Apply when context is filling up: large outputs, long files, repeated reads, fan-out planning. - **nh-principle-make-operations-idempotent**: Apply when designing commands, lifecycle steps, or processing loops that run amid crashes, restarts, and retries. - **nh-principle-migrate-callers-then-delete-legacy-apis**: Apply when introducing a new internal API while old callers still exist. ([neohaskell/skills](https://github.com/neohaskell/skills/tree/HEAD/skills/nh-principle-migrate-callers-then-delete-legacy-apis))
- **nh-principle-minimal-change**: Apply when refactoring, evaluating diff size, or tempted to add abstractions, layers, or signal threading. - **nh-principle-minimize-reader-load**: Apply when reviewing or shaping code that's hard to trace. - **nh-principle-model-the-domain**: Apply when writing stateful logic, or when code branches a lot or repeats a shape assumption across files. ([neohaskell/skills](https://github.com/neohaskell/skills/tree/HEAD/skills/nh-principle-model-the-domain))
- **nh-principle-name-for-the-reader**: Choose NeoHaskell module, type, and operation names that a junior TypeScript developer can understand without knowing GHC implementation terms. ([neohaskell/skills](https://github.com/neohaskell/skills/tree/HEAD/skills/nh-principle-name-for-the-reader))
- **nh-principle-never-block-on-the-human**: Apply when tempted to ask 'should I do X?' on reversible work. ([neohaskell/skills](https://github.com/neohaskell/skills/tree/HEAD/skills/nh-principle-never-block-on-the-human))
